It would be unfair to penalize them for high positive deviation, so the Sortino ratio focuses on downside risk. This is particularly useful in comparing the performance of hedge funds.

410. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §8.6.3] What is a bogey portfolio?

  • a. A portfolio with zero risk
  • b. The market portfolio
  • c. A portfolio that has underperformed
  • d. A benchmark designed to measure returns if a passive strategy were adopted

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. A benchmark designed to measure returns if a passive strategy were adopted

Rationale: Part of the analysis process involves identifying a performance benchmark, called the bogey, for comparing the portfolio's results. The bogey is designed to measure returns if a passive strategy were adopted.

411. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §8.6.3] A managed portfolio returns 5.55% and the bogey returns 4.10%. What is the excess return?

  • a. 1.45%
  • b. 1.10%
  • c. 0.35%
  • d. 9.65%

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. 1.45%

Rationale: Excess return = 5.55% − 4.10% = 1.45%. This is then decomposed into asset allocation and selection effects.

412. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §8.6.3] The managed portfolio holds 70% equities against a benchmark weight of 60%, and the benchmark equity return is 6%. What is the asset allocation contribution from equities?

  • a. 0.10%
  • b. 0.60%
  • c. 1.05%
  • d. 0.35%

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. 0.60%

Rationale: Asset allocation contribution = (Actual weight − Benchmark weight) × Benchmark return = (0.70 − 0.60) × 6% = 0.10 × 6% = 0.60%.

413. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §8.6.3] The managed portfolio holds 70% equities with an actual return of 7.50% against a benchmark return of 6.00%. What is the selection contribution from equities?

  • a. 1.50%
  • b. 0.35%
  • c. 1.05%
  • d. 0.60%

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. 1.05%

Rationale: Selection contribution = Actual weight × (Actual return − Benchmark return) = 0.70 × (7.50% − 6.00%) = 0.70 × 1.50% = 1.05%.

414. [Multiple response] [CM-EIP §8.6.3] Which of the following are characteristics of a good benchmark? (Select all that apply)

  • a. Unambiguous, with the asset class and composition clearly stated
  • b. Investable as a passively managed portfolio
  • c. Measurable, with returns obtainable from the market
  • d. Chosen by the client without the manager's agreement

Answer and explanation

Answer: A, B, C

Rationale: Useful benchmarks should be unambiguous, investable, measurable, consistent with the investment manager's investment philosophy, and bought in by the investment manager as an acceptable benchmark.

415. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §8.7] What is a retrocession in the context of fund sales?

  • a. A tax rebate
  • b. A discount on the offer price
  • c. A refund of management fees to investors
  • d. A payment to third-party vendors for promoting the fund

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. A payment to third-party vendors for promoting the fund

Rationale: The sales force should ensure there is no misrepresentation about the reliance on third-party vendors, who receive payments such as retrocessions for promoting the fund. Mis-selling can result when there is poor communication and a lack of enforcement of required practices.

Chapter 9 — Exchange Traded Funds (ETFs), Unit Trusts, Real Estate Investment Trusts (REITs) and Insurance

416. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.1] What are the two key features of a Variable Capital Company?

  • a. It can freely issue and redeem shares and pay dividends from capital, and can adopt an umbrella structure with sub-funds
  • b. It must be listed on an exchange and cannot have sub-funds
  • c. It is taxed as a partnership and must disclose its shareholder register
  • d. It can only be used for closed-end funds and cannot pay dividends

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. It can freely issue and redeem shares and pay dividends from capital, and can adopt an umbrella structure with sub-funds

Rationale: A VCC can freely issue and redeem shares and pay dividends using its capital. A VCC can also adopt an umbrella structure with multiple sub-funds, which increases operating efficiency.

417. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2] How does an ETF differ from a typical mutual fund in terms of trading?

  • a. Both trade identically
  • b. ETF units can be bought and sold during trading hours like any listed security; mutual funds can only be bought or sold at the end of the day
  • c. ETFs can only be bought at the end of the day
  • d. Mutual funds trade continuously

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. ETF units can be bought and sold during trading hours like any listed security; mutual funds can only be bought or sold at the end of the day

Rationale: ETF units can be bought and sold by investors during trading hours like any security listed on the Exchange. This is unlike mutual funds, which can only be bought or sold at the end of the day with the mutual fund manager.

418. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §9.2] The SPDR STI ETF is priced at about 1/100th of the STI level. If the STI is at 1,700, what is the price of one unit?

  • a. $0.17
  • b. $1.70
  • c. $17.00
  • d. $170.00

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. $17.00

Rationale: Pricing is about 1/100th of the STI level. If the STI is at 1,700, each unit of STI ETF 100 will be priced at $17.00.

419. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2] In what size can units of the STI ETF be created for or redeemed by participating dealers?

  • a. 1,000,000 units
  • b. 1,000 units
  • c. 10,000 units
  • d. 100,000 units

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. 100,000 units

Rationale: Unlike a typical unit trust that creates units for investors when they purchase units, units of the STI ETF can only be created for, or redeemed by, participating dealers in sizes of 100,000 units.

420. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2] What happens when an ETF's traded price is above its net asset value?

  • a. An arbitrageur sells the ETF shares and buys the underlying portfolio of securities
  • b. An arbitrageur buys the ETF shares and sells the underlying portfolio
  • c. Nothing happens
  • d. The ETF is delisted

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. An arbitrageur sells the ETF shares and buys the underlying portfolio of securities

Rationale: When the traded price of an ETF is higher than its NAV, an arbitrageur will sell the ETF shares and buy the underlying portfolio of securities. If the traded price falls below the NAV, the arbitrageur will sell the underlying portfolio and buy the ETF shares.

421. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.1] What is representative sampling in a physical ETF?

  • a. Holding all index constituents in exact proportions
  • b. Investing in a subset of the index comprising the more dominant stocks, used when constituents are not liquid
  • c. Using swaps to replicate the index
  • d. Holding only cash

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Investing in a subset of the index comprising the more dominant stocks, used when constituents are not liquid

Rationale: Representative sampling is where the ETF invests in a subset of the index, comprising the more dominant stocks. This method is used when the stocks that make up the index are not liquid. Full replication purchases all of the listed stocks in the exact proportions.

422. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.1] What is the main advantage and disadvantage of synthetic replication?

  • a. Advantage: physical ownership. Disadvantage: illiquidity
  • b. Advantage: lower cost. Disadvantage: higher tracking error
  • c. Advantage: reduced tracking error. Disadvantage: counterparty risk
  • d. Advantage: no counterparty risk. Disadvantage: higher tracking error

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Advantage: reduced tracking error. Disadvantage: counterparty risk

Rationale: One advantage of synthetic replication is that it reduces the error of the ETF in tracking the index. A disadvantage is counterparty risk, as the counterparty to the derivative contract might default on its obligations.

423. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.2] What is cash equitisation?

  • a. Hedging a portfolio with cash
  • b. Investing only in money market funds
  • c. Converting equities to cash
  • d. Converting cash to equities through investing in ETFs, to minimise the cash drag on a portfolio

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Converting cash to equities through investing in ETFs, to minimise the cash drag on a portfolio

Rationale: Cash equitisation is converting cash to equities through investing in ETFs, to minimise the cash drag on a portfolio. Cash drag is the underperformance of a portfolio from holding idle cash.

424. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.3] What collateral might an ETF receive for lending out shares?

  • a. Cash or other securities equivalent to about 105% of the stocks' value
  • b. A guarantee from the exchange
  • c. A government bond at 50% of value
  • d. Nothing

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Cash or other securities equivalent to about 105% of the stocks' value

Rationale: An ETF can transfer the stocks to another investor over the lending period in exchange for collateral such as cash or other securities equivalent to, for example, 105% of the stocks' value. If the borrower cannot fulfil its obligations, the ETF faces a loss equal to the value of the lent stocks.

425. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.4] How do ETF annual management fees compare with actively managed unit trusts?

  • a. ETFs charge more
  • b. ETFs normally charge less than 1%, compared with 1–2% for actively managed unit trusts
  • c. Both charge the same
  • d. ETFs charge no fees at all

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. ETFs normally charge less than 1%, compared with 1–2% for actively managed unit trusts

Rationale: The annual management fees for ETFs are normally less than 1% compared to the 1–2% that is charged by actively managed unit trusts. In addition, there are no initial charges or redemption fees when trading ETFs.

426. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.5] In which type of ETF is tracking error greater?

  • a. Neither has tracking error
  • b. Synthetic ETFs
  • c. Physical ETFs
  • d. Both equally

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Physical ETFs

Rationale: Tracking error is greater in physical ETFs than in synthetic ETFs, principally because synthetic ETFs can achieve more precise tracking using swaps and derivatives.

427. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.5] Which tax risk applies to non-US investors holding US-established ETFs?

  • a. No tax applies
  • b. Capital gains tax
  • c. Singapore withholding tax
  • d. US withholding taxes on dividends and other distributions

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. US withholding taxes on dividends and other distributions

Rationale: Non-US investors holding US-established ETFs may be subject to US withholding taxes on dividends and other distributions.

428. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.6] How does a smart beta ETF differ from a standard ETF?

  • a. It selects and weights securities on positive performance criteria rather than market capitalisation
  • b. It only holds bonds
  • c. It has no index
  • d. It is actively managed by a stock picker

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. It selects and weights securities on positive performance criteria rather than market capitalisation

Rationale: In contrast to a standard ETF that typically follows an index's market capitalization weighting, a smart beta ETF will select and weight securities based on some positive performance criteria, such as improvements to cashflow, dividends or revenue/earnings ratios.

429. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.7] Why is holding a long volatility ETF position over time described as potentially deadly?

  • a. Because volatility always rises
  • b. Because volatility trends downward, and a 1–2% fall each day produces massive losses over time
  • c. Because the ETF is delisted
  • d. Because the ETF pays no dividends

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Because volatility trends downward, and a 1–2% fall each day produces massive losses over time

Rationale: Holding on to a long volatility position to wait for the upward spike can be deadly as volatility trends downward, and a 1–2% fall each day will soon lead to massive losses over time. High volatility generally does not last, and the beneficiaries tend to be those that shorted volatility.

430. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.8] What leverage multiple do leveraged ETFs typically use?

  • a. 20x or more
  • b. 0.5x or 1x
  • c. 2x or 3x
  • d. 5x or 10x

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. 2x or 3x

Rationale: Such ETFs are usually leveraged 2x or 3x the index value and there are also those that track the inverse movement of the underlying index. Leveraged ETFs are often issued in pairs, long and short a particular index.

431. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.2.8] Why do issuers of leveraged ETFs often carry out reverse splits?

  • a. To reduce the leverage
  • b. To change the index tracked
  • c. To raise new capital
  • d. To reset prices at higher levels after sharp movements

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. To reset prices at higher levels after sharp movements

Rationale: Due to the sharp movements of leveraged ETFs, issuers of leveraged ETFs often carry out reverse splits to reset prices at higher levels. Hence regular price resetting takes place for many of the popular ETFs.

432. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3] What is a unit trust in the United States called?

  • a. A mutual fund
  • b. An investment fund
  • c. A unit trust
  • d. A Sicav

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. A mutual fund

Rationale: In the US, a CIS is known as a mutual fund and may not be issued in the form of a trust. In the UK the term unit trust is actively used; in Germany it is an investment fund; in Switzerland and Luxembourg the terms are Sicaf and Sicav.

433. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.1] How many places of domicile can a fund have?

  • a. Unlimited
  • b. One
  • c. Two
  • d. As many as it is registered in

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. One

Rationale: The domicile of a fund is where the fund is based and set up. A fund can be registered in multiple jurisdictions, but it can only have one place of domicile.

434. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.3] How is the price of a closed-end unit trust determined?

  • a. By the MAS
  • b. By net asset value only
  • c. By market demand and supply as well as the performance of the fund
  • d. By the trustee

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. By market demand and supply as well as the performance of the fund

Rationale: For an open-end trust the price is determined by net asset value, the performance of the fund net of management fees. For a closed-end trust the price is determined by market demand and supply as well as the performance of the fund.

435. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §9.3.3] A unit trust's investments are worth $10 million and there are 5 million units issued. What is the value of each unit?

  • a. $0.50
  • b. $2.00
  • c. $5.00
  • d. $10.00

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. $2.00

Rationale: Each unit represents the same proportion of the value of the assets held by the trust. $10 million / 5 million units = $2 per unit.

436. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.4] Who holds a unit trust's cash, shares and other investment assets?

  • a. The distributor
  • b. The custodian bank's shareholders
  • c. The fund manager
  • d. The trustee

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. The trustee

Rationale: The managers neither hold the investments nor issue or redeem units. This is done by the trustee, who holds the trust's cash, shares and other investment assets.

437. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.5] What is the typical initial investment outlay for a unit trust quoted in the guide?

  • a. $1,000 to $5,000
  • b. $10,000 to $50,000
  • c. $100,000 or more
  • d. $100 to $500

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. $1,000 to $5,000

Rationale: The initial investment outlay, for example $1,000 to $5,000, is affordable to individual investors. For this amount, the average investor can invest in a portfolio of securities that would otherwise be out of his reach.

438. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.6] Which is a disadvantage of investing in unit trusts?

  • a. No diversification
  • b. Sales charges on entry and management fees paid regardless of performance
  • c. No professional management
  • d. No liquidity

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Sales charges on entry and management fees paid regardless of performance

Rationale: Relatively high sales charges are incurred when an investor buys into a unit trust, and unit trusts pay annual management fees to the fund manager regardless of the fund's performance.

439. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.7] How do capital guaranteed funds differ from capital preservation funds?

  • a. Capital guaranteed funds cannot be sold
  • b. They are identical
  • c. Capital guaranteed funds have a guarantor and guarantee only if held to maturity; capital preservation funds have no guarantor and use less onerous language
  • d. Capital preservation funds have a guarantor

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Capital guaranteed funds have a guarantor and guarantee only if held to maturity; capital preservation funds have no guarantor and use less onerous language

Rationale: Capital guaranteed funds invest most capital in low-risk assets to preserve capital at maturity, and the guarantee usually applies only if held to maturity. Capital preservation funds aim to provide some level of capital preservation, the language used is less onerous for the issuer, and the fund does not require a guarantor.

440. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.7] Which fund is described as the best-known example of a quantitative fund?

  • a. Fidelity Magellan
  • b. Vanguard 500
  • c. Berkshire Hathaway
  • d. Long-Term Capital Management

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Long-Term Capital Management

Rationale: Quant funds use price and market modelling based on complex mathematical algorithms. Quite probably the best known example is Long-Term Capital Management, whose board of directors included Myron S. Scholes and Robert C. Merton.

441. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.7] Why is the name hedge fund described as a misnomer?

  • a. Because hedge funds are not necessarily hedged and may take directional bets, being net long or short
  • b. Because hedge funds only invest in bonds
  • c. Because hedge funds are always market neutral
  • d. Because hedge funds never use derivatives

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Because hedge funds are not necessarily hedged and may take directional bets, being net long or short

Rationale: The name hedge fund can be a misnomer as hedge funds are not necessarily hedged. For example, a hedge fund manager may take directional bets on the markets, that is they can be net long or short.

442. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.8] Which of the following appears on a monthly fund fact sheet?

  • a. The trust deed
  • b. Top holdings, country allocation, sector allocation and risk statistics
  • c. The auditor's report
  • d. The board of directors

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Top holdings, country allocation, sector allocation and risk statistics

Rationale: Summary information on the monthly fund fact sheets includes fund objective, key information on fees, performance data and ratios, benchmark comparison, top holdings, country allocation, risk statistics and sector allocation.

443. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.10] What is the typical range of a front-end load?

  • a. 10% to 20% of NAV
  • b. 0.1% to 0.5% of NAV
  • c. 1.5% to 5% of NAV
  • d. 5% to 10% of NAV

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. 1.5% to 5% of NAV

Rationale: The load often serves as a commission for the services of the distributors of the unit trust and the front-end load is usually 1.5 to 5% of the NAV.

444. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.10] What is the typical range of a unit trust annual management fee?

  • a. 2.0% to 5.0% of NAV
  • b. 5.0% to 10.0% of NAV
  • c. 0.1% to 0.15% of NAV
  • d. 0.5% to 2.0% of NAV

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. 0.5% to 2.0% of NAV

Rationale: All unit trusts charge annual management fees to compensate their professional managers. This fee typically ranges from 0.5% to 2.0% of the NAV and is accrued daily and debited directly from the unit trust's asset value.

445. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.10] What is the purpose of a high watermark in a performance fee arrangement?

  • a. To ensure the manager generates a certain level of profit before the bonus can be taken, so it does not unfairly benefit from a fund recovering after a fall
  • b. To set the minimum investment
  • c. To determine the offer price
  • d. To cap the manager's total fee

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. To ensure the manager generates a certain level of profit before the bonus can be taken, so it does not unfairly benefit from a fund recovering after a fall

Rationale: There are measures to ensure that a fund manager does not unfairly benefit from a fund that recovers after first falling sharply. A certain performance level, the high watermark, is set to ensure that the fund manager generates a certain level of profits for the fund before the performance bonus can be taken out of the NAV.

446. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.11] What benchmark might a fund with an absolute return target use?

  • a. A broad equity index
  • b. An interbank rate such as LIBOR plus 1%
  • c. A bond index
  • d. No benchmark at all

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. An interbank rate such as LIBOR plus 1%

Rationale: For traditional funds with a relative return target, the benchmark is usually an index. For funds with absolute return targets, an interbank rate, for example LIBOR plus 1%, might be the benchmark target to beat.

447. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §9.3.12] A fund has securities of $100m, other assets of $5m, liabilities including management fees of $3m, no leverage, and 10m units outstanding. What is the NAV per unit?

  • a. $9.70
  • b. $10.00
  • c. $10.20
  • d. $10.50

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. $10.20

Rationale: NAV = (Fund securities + Other fund assets − Liabilities − Debt used for leveraging) / Total fund units outstanding = (100 + 5 − 3 − 0) / 10 = 102/10 = $10.20.

448. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.13] Which expenses are added when calculating the offer price of a unit trust?

  • a. Only the trustee fee
  • b. Only the audit fee
  • c. Expenses related to the sale of securities
  • d. Expenses related to the purchase of securities, such as stockbrokers' commission and clearing fees

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Expenses related to the purchase of securities, such as stockbrokers' commission and clearing fees

Rationale: The offer price is the price at which an investor can buy units. The steps are: determine NAV per unit, add expenses related to purchase of securities such as stockbrokers' commission and clearing fees, add cash held by the trust and accrued income, and add the manager's initial charge.

449. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.14] What is the typical maximum single-security holding in many funds, and how high may some managers allow it to rise?

  • a. 5%, rising to 8–9%
  • b. 10%, rising to 20%
  • c. 20%, rising to 50%
  • d. 1%, rising to 2%

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. 5%, rising to 8–9%

Rationale: Many funds do not allow a single security to constitute more than 5% of total asset holdings, but some managers may allow the fund's exposure to a particular security to rise as high as 8–9% in an attempt to outperform the market.

450. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.17] Which unit trusts are the exception to the general rule that unit trusts suit medium to long-term investors?

  • a. Money market funds
  • b. Leveraged ETFs and highly volatile commodity ETFs, which are meant more for traders
  • c. Bond funds
  • d. Balanced funds

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Leveraged ETFs and highly volatile commodity ETFs, which are meant more for traders

Rationale: Generally, unit trusts are better suited for investors with medium to long-term investment horizons, the exception being certain leveraged ETFs and highly volatile commodity ETFs, which are meant more for traders.

451. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §9.3.17] A SGD 10,000 legal expense is billed to a fund. What proportion of the fund is it for an SGD 8 million fund and an SGD 1 million fund?

  • a. 0.0125% and 0.1%
  • b. 0.5% and 5%
  • c. 0.125% and 1%
  • d. 1% and 0.125%

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. 0.125% and 1%

Rationale: A SGD 10,000 legal expense billed to an SGD 8 million fund is 0.125% of the fund, but billed to an SGD 1 million fund it is 1% of the fund. A small fund size can therefore materially affect performance.

452. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.3.18] Why do retrocessions create a conflict of interest?

  • a. Because they increase the fund's NAV
  • b. Because they are illegal everywhere
  • c. Because they are paid by the investor
  • d. Because the distributor recommending the fund receives a fee from the fund house, compromising the independence of the advice

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Because the distributor recommending the fund receives a fee from the fund house, compromising the independence of the advice

Rationale: Retrocessions are fees received by banks and other financial institutions for distributing third-party products to clients. When recommendations of funds are made to new clients, there could be a conflict of interest and the independence of the investment advisors are compromised. Retrocession fees are also a cost offset against fund performance, negatively affecting the NAV.

453. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.4.1] What minimum proportion of a REIT must be invested in income-producing real estate?

  • a. 45%
  • b. 75%
  • c. 90%
  • d. 100%

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. 75%

Rationale: A REIT is a unit trust that is predominantly invested in real estate and real estate-related assets. Specifically, the Code on CIS requires a minimum of at least 75% of the fund to be invested in income-producing real estate.

454. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.4.1] How often must REIT properties be valued under the Code on CIS?

  • a. Once a year
  • b. Every five years
  • c. Daily
  • d. Monthly

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Once a year

Rationale: Unlike equities and bonds in unit trusts where current market values can readily be obtained from the stock exchanges or bond dealers, the Code on CIS only requires the properties on a REIT to be valued once a year.

455. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.4.2] What tax advantage do REITs have over direct property investment?

  • a. REITs pay no tax at all
  • b. REITs distributing at least 90% of taxable income enjoy favourable tax treatment, with no corresponding advantage for direct investment
  • c. Direct property is tax-exempt
  • d. Both are equally taxed

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. REITs distributing at least 90% of taxable income enjoy favourable tax treatment, with no corresponding advantage for direct investment

Rationale: Since REITs that distribute at least 90% of their taxable income each year enjoy favourable tax treatment, many of them do; there is no corresponding tax advantage for direct investments.

456. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.4.3] What is the single-tier leverage limit for Singapore REITs?

  • a. 25%
  • b. 35%
  • c. 45%
  • d. No limit

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. 45%

Rationale: In Singapore, REITs are subject to a single-tier leverage limit of 45%. The need to service the debt used to finance the acquisition of underlying properties implies less income for distribution and greater volatility in the equity value of the REIT.

457. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.4.3] Why do REITs face refinancing risk?

  • a. Because their properties are illiquid
  • b. Because they have no tenants
  • c. Because they cannot borrow
  • d. Because they distribute at least 90% of income, so cannot easily build cash reserves for debt repayment and must refinance or raise new equity

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Because they distribute at least 90% of income, so cannot easily build cash reserves for debt repayment and must refinance or raise new equity

Rationale: As REITs typically distribute at least 90% of their income annually to unit holders, it is not easy for them to build up cash reserves for debt repayment, so they typically resort to fresh borrowing or raising new equity. If unable to make these arrangements, a REIT may be forced to liquidate some investments.

458. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.5.1] Is a business trust a separate legal entity?

  • a. No, unlike a company it is not a separate legal entity
  • b. Only if listed
  • c. Only if it has a trustee
  • d. Yes, like a company

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. No, unlike a company it is not a separate legal entity

Rationale: A business trust is a hybrid structure with characteristics of both companies and trusts but, unlike a company, a business trust is not a separate legal entity.

459. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.5.1] What is a trustee-manager in a Singapore business trust?

  • a. Two separate entities
  • b. A single responsible entity vested with the roles of both trustee and manager of the trust assets
  • c. A government regulator
  • d. The unitholders' committee

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. A single responsible entity vested with the roles of both trustee and manager of the trust assets

Rationale: In Singapore, business trusts are a single responsible entity organization and the trustee-manager is vested with the roles of both trustee and manager of the trust assets. The trustee-manager has legal ownership of the trust's assets.

460. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.5.2] Why is a business trust structure attractive to infrastructure providers?

  • a. Because they must distribute 90% of income
  • b. Because they pay no tax
  • c. Because distributions can be paid out of operating cash flow, which matters when depreciating assets reduce accounting profits despite strong cash flows
  • d. Because they have a 45% gearing limit

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Because distributions can be paid out of operating cash flow, which matters when depreciating assets reduce accounting profits despite strong cash flows

Rationale: The main attractive feature of business trusts is their effectiveness in unlocking value from fixed assets that generate cash flow. Such businesses usually have depreciating assets that reduce accounting profits despite strong positive cashflows, which makes business trust structures attractive because dividends can be paid out of operating cash flow.

461. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.5.4] How does the gearing limit of a business trust compare with a REIT?

  • a. Business trusts are limited to 45%; REITs have no limit
  • b. Both have no limit
  • c. Both are 45%
  • d. Business trusts have no limit; REITs are limited to 45%

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Business trusts have no limit; REITs are limited to 45%

Rationale: There is no gearing limit for business trusts but a 45% gearing limit for REITs. However, debt could still become a problem if the business trust uses that freedom for indiscriminate borrowing.

462. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.5.4] What distribution requirement applies to a listed REIT compared with a business trust?

  • a. A REIT must distribute a minimum of 90% of annual income; a business trust has no mandatory minimum
  • b. A business trust must distribute 90%; a REIT has no minimum
  • c. Neither has a minimum
  • d. Both must distribute 90%

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. A REIT must distribute a minimum of 90% of annual income; a business trust has no mandatory minimum

Rationale: A listed REIT has a mandatory distribution of a minimum of 90% of annual income to qualify for favourable tax treatment. A business trust has no mandatory minimum and can distribute from operating cash flow.

463. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.6] Why can a stapled security combining a REIT and a business trust not be marketed as a REIT?

  • a. Because it is unlisted
  • b. Because the issuer is bound by the regulations specific to each security, and such stapled securities cannot be marketed as REITs
  • c. Because it has no income
  • d. Because it has no trustee

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Because the issuer is bound by the regulations specific to each security, and such stapled securities cannot be marketed as REITs

Rationale: In a stapled security, the issuer is bound by the regulations specific to each of the securities being stapled. Such stapled securities cannot be marketed as REITs.

464. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.7] What does UCITS provide for funds?

  • a. A single currency
  • b. A tax exemption
  • c. A passport for funds to be sold internationally
  • d. A guarantee of returns

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. A passport for funds to be sold internationally

Rationale: The UCITS provides the passport for funds to be sold internationally. The original objectives were to remove the barriers to cross-border distribution and marketing of funds by allowing funds to invest in a wider range of financial instruments.

465. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.7] Since when has MAS allowed UCITS funds to be distributed and sold in Singapore?

  • a. 1998
  • b. 2002
  • c. 2006
  • d. 2013

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. 2006

Rationale: Since 2006, MAS has allowed UCITS funds to be distributed and sold in Singapore if these meet the local regulatory conditions.

466. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8] What is the main benefit of alternative investments?

  • a. They are risk-free
  • b. They are always liquid
  • c. They always outperform equities
  • d. They can further diversify the portfolio using asset classes less correlated with traditional equities and fixed income

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. They can further diversify the portfolio using asset classes less correlated with traditional equities and fixed income

Rationale: The main benefit of investing in alternative investments is the ability to further diversify the portfolio using asset classes that are less correlated with the traditional asset classes of equities and fixed income. Through such diversification, the portfolio's risk-adjusted returns may potentially be increased.

467. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8] Do alternative investments provide a buffer during major financial crises?

  • a. Not necessarily, because they are also affected by a widespread massive sell-off of assets
  • b. Only hedge funds do
  • c. Only private equity does
  • d. Yes, always

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Not necessarily, because they are also affected by a widespread massive sell-off of assets

Rationale: During major financial crises, such as the Great Financial Crisis of 2008, investing in alternative investments do not necessarily provide much of a buffer since such investments are also affected by a widespread massive sell-off of assets. However, under normal market conditions, alternative investments have been found to be less correlated with equity and bonds.

468. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.2] What do hedge funds capture that conventional funds largely cannot?

  • a. Beta only
  • b. Alpha, with the freedom to hedge away or reduce beta
  • c. Only market return
  • d. Only dividends

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Alpha, with the freedom to hedge away or reduce beta

Rationale: Hedge funds capture alpha, with the freedom to hedge away or reduce beta. Conventional funds capture alpha plus beta, with limited ability to reduce beta risk.

469. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.3] What is basis risk in a hedge fund context?

  • a. The risk of currency movements
  • b. The risk that the fund closes
  • c. The risk that remains as a result of an imperfect hedge because a complete offsetting position may not exist
  • d. The risk of rising interest rates

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. The risk that remains as a result of an imperfect hedge because a complete offsetting position may not exist

Rationale: Basis risk is the risk that remains as a result of an imperfect hedge because a complete offsetting position may not exist. Counterparty risk arises when the other party to a derivative transaction does not make good on its contractual obligation.

470. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.3] What is a gate provision in a hedge fund?

  • a. A minimum investment
  • b. A lock-up on new investors
  • c. An entry fee
  • d. A provision allowing the manager to limit withdrawals on a given withdrawal date to a percentage of the fund's net assets

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. A provision allowing the manager to limit withdrawals on a given withdrawal date to a percentage of the fund's net assets

Rationale: Many hedge funds have lock-up periods during which investors are not allowed to redeem their investment, and some have gate provisions, which allow the manager to limit the withdrawals on a given withdrawal date to a percentage of the fund's net assets.

471. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.4] What is the rationale for convertible arbitrage?

  • a. Convertible securities are usually slower to reprice to fair value than the underlying stock, creating arbitrage opportunities
  • b. Convertibles pay no interest
  • c. Shares are always overvalued
  • d. Convertibles always outperform shares

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Convertible securities are usually slower to reprice to fair value than the underlying stock, creating arbitrage opportunities

Rationale: This strategy involves the purchase of the convertible securities while shorting the underlying stock. The rationale is that, for a variety of reasons, convertible securities are usually slower in repricing to fair value as compared to the underlying stock, which leads to arbitrage opportunities.

472. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.5] At what price range can distressed bonds trade, depending on the severity of the company's crisis?

  • a. 90–99% of par
  • b. 10–99% of par
  • c. 1–10% of par
  • d. Always at par

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. 10–99% of par

Rationale: Depending on the degree of the crisis facing a particular company, the distressed bonds can be priced at 10% to 99% of par. Hedge funds purchase distressed debt and borrowings that they believe to be undervalued.

473. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.6] What do global macro strategies take?

  • a. Only positions in government bonds
  • b. Only long positions
  • c. Directional views on financial products and derivatives in anticipation of market reactions to economic or political changes
  • d. Only market-neutral positions

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Directional views on financial products and derivatives in anticipation of market reactions to economic or political changes

Rationale: Global macro strategies take directional views on various financial products and their derivatives in anticipation of the market reaction from significant economic or political changes in one or more countries.

474. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.7] What do directional quantitative trading strategies mainly follow?

  • a. Central bank announcements
  • b. Dividend announcements
  • c. Fundamental valuation
  • d. Technical price trends and momentum

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Technical price trends and momentum

Rationale: Directional quantitative trading strategies employ a combination of technical analysis, market data and funds flow to make investment decisions. These strategies mainly follow technical pricing trends and momentum.

475. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.8] What happens to returns when a fund of hedge funds diversifies across strategies?

  • a. They typically fall to the average return achieved by hedge funds
  • b. They become risk-free
  • c. They are unaffected
  • d. They rise above the best manager

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. They typically fall to the average return achieved by hedge funds

Rationale: With diversification, returns will typically fall to the average return achieved by hedge funds, which may not fit the expectation that some investors have when investing in hedge funds.

476. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.10] Which private equity stage provides seed and start-up capital in the early stages of a business?

  • a. Growth capital
  • b. Venture capital
  • c. Buyout funds
  • d. Distressed businesses

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Venture capital

Rationale: Venture capitalists co-invest with an entrepreneur to provide seed and/or start-up capital in the early stages of a business. In return for the higher risk, they expect a higher-than-average return on their investment.

477. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.10] Which private equity stage invests in mature companies with stable cash flows and established products?

  • a. Venture capital
  • b. Growth capital
  • c. Buyout funds
  • d. Special situations

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Buyout funds

Rationale: Buyout funds invest in mature companies with stable cash flows, established products and developed personnel. The buyout could occur pre-IPO for owners who want to exit before the IPO process.

478. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.11] How long is the investment period of a private equity fund, and when does the harvest period typically begin?

  • a. 10 years; harvest from year 11
  • b. 2–3 years; harvest from year 4
  • c. 1–2 years; harvest from year 3
  • d. 5–6 years; harvest from year 6

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. 5–6 years; harvest from year 6

Rationale: The first 5–6 years represent the investment period. The harvest period typically begins from the 6th year onwards when the fund will start making payouts.

479. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.11] What internal rate of return is typically expected from private equity?

  • a. 10–15%
  • b. 25–30%
  • c. 2–4%
  • d. 5–8%

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. 10–15%

Rationale: The incentive for investing in private equity is that the expected internal rate of return would typically be in the double digits, 10–15%. The downside is that the funds would be locked up for several years.

480. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.12] Why do agriculture commodity funds suffer long downward cycles?

  • a. Because demand always falls
  • b. Because successes in agricultural production supply often outstrip demand, causing overhanging supply
  • c. Because of government regulation
  • d. Because of futures market rules

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Because successes in agricultural production supply often outstrip demand, causing overhanging supply

Rationale: Agriculture commodity funds suffer from the same problem as their underlying commodities: the successes in agriculture production supply often outstrip demand, causing long downward cycles of overhanging supply.

481. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.8.12] What is the main concern when investing in real estate funds?

  • a. The fund manager's fees
  • b. The level of rental income
  • c. The mismatch between unit liquidity and the illiquidity of the underlying assets
  • d. The tax treatment

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. The mismatch between unit liquidity and the illiquidity of the underlying assets

Rationale: As with REITs, the main concern when investing in real estate funds is the mismatch between unit liquidity and the illiquidity of the underlying assets. In a property bubble situation, asset prices can fall very sharply once the bubble has burst, leading to redemptions.

482. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.9.1] When is the sum assured of a whole life insurance policy payable?

  • a. Only if the policy is surrendered
  • b. After 10 years
  • c. Only at a fixed maturity date
  • d. On the death of the life insured, whenever this takes place

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. On the death of the life insured, whenever this takes place

Rationale: In the case of a whole life insurance policy, the sum assured will be payable on the death of the life insured, whenever this takes place. Policy owners pay fixed monthly or annual premiums and beneficiaries receive fixed death benefits.

483. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.9.2] When is the sum assured of an endowment policy payable?

  • a. On the maturity date or on death, whichever is earlier
  • b. Only at maturity
  • c. Only on surrender
  • d. Only on death

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. On the maturity date or on death, whichever is earlier

Rationale: Endowment insurance policies usually mature after a fixed period of time, for example 10, 15 or 20 years, and the sum assured is payable on the maturity date or on the life insured's death, whichever is earlier.

484. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.9.2] Why may the guaranteed cash value of an endowment policy be less than the sum of premiums paid?

  • a. Because of tax
  • b. Because part of the premiums pays for insurance protection while the rest is invested and subject to investment risk
  • c. Because of agent commission only
  • d. Because the insurer keeps the difference

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Because part of the premiums pays for insurance protection while the rest is invested and subject to investment risk

Rationale: The guaranteed cash values received may be less than the sum of the premiums paid, because part of the premiums will pay for insurance protection while the rest is invested and subject to investment risks.

485. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.9.3] How are benefits linked to investment performance in a with-profits policy?

  • a. Only through the stock market
  • b. Directly, through unit values
  • c. Indirectly, through annual valuation of the life fund and a reversionary bonus decided by the board
  • d. Not at all

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Indirectly, through annual valuation of the life fund and a reversionary bonus decided by the board

Rationale: Under a with-profits policy, the link between the policy benefits and the investment and operating performance of the insurer is not direct and depends on the annual valuation of the fund assets and liabilities. The allocation of the surplus as a reversionary bonus ultimately depends on the decision of the board of directors.

486. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.9.3] How do investment-linked policies differ from with-profits policies?

  • a. They guarantee a fixed return
  • b. They cannot be surrendered
  • c. They have no investment element
  • d. Policy values are directly linked to the investment performance of underlying units and fluctuate daily

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Policy values are directly linked to the investment performance of underlying units and fluctuate daily

Rationale: Investment-linked life insurance policies offer policies with values that are directly linked to the investment performance of the underlying instruments, usually by formally linking the values of the policies to units in a fund. The investment element fluctuates daily according to the performance of those investments.

487. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.10.1] How is an immediate annuity purchased?

  • a. With a lump sum, a single premium
  • b. With a combination of premiums and equity
  • c. It cannot be purchased directly
  • d. With periodic premiums only

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. With a lump sum, a single premium

Rationale: An immediate annuity is a contract where payments to the annuitant begin as soon as it is purchased. An immediate annuity is always purchased with a lump-sum, a single premium.

488. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.10.1] On what does the amount received from a deferred annuity depend?

  • a. Only on the contributions
  • b. On contributions, interest earned, the annuitant's gender and the annuitant's age when payments begin
  • c. Only on the annuitant's age
  • d. Only on the insurer's profits

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. On contributions, interest earned, the annuitant's gender and the annuitant's age when payments begin

Rationale: The amount that the annuitant will periodically receive depends on his contributions, the interest earned on the contributions, the annuitant's gender, and the annuitant's age when the payments begin.

489. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §9.10.2] What is the present value of $1,000 paid every year for 5 years at a 3% discount rate?

  • a. $4,212.36
  • b. $5,309.14
  • c. $4,579.71
  • d. $5,000.00

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. $4,579.71

Rationale: PV = C × [1 − (1 + i)^−n] / i = 1,000 × [1 − (1.03)^−5] / 0.03 = 1,000 × [1 − 0.86261] / 0.03 = 1,000 × 4.57971 = $4,579.71.

490. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §9.10.2] What is the future value of $1,000 paid every year for 8 years at 5%?

  • a. $10,000.00
  • b. $8,142.00
  • c. $8,000.00
  • d. $9,549.11

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. $9,549.11

Rationale: FV = C × [(1 + i)^n − 1] / i = 1,000 × [(1.05)^8 − 1] / 0.05 = 1,000 × [1.47746 − 1] / 0.05 = 1,000 × 9.54911 = $9,549.11.

491. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.10.3] What risk does an annuity protect against, and how does it compare with life insurance?

  • a. Excessive longevity; the opposite of life insurance, which protects against premature death
  • b. Inflation; the same as life insurance
  • c. Interest rate risk; unrelated to life insurance
  • d. Premature death; the same as life insurance

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Excessive longevity; the opposite of life insurance, which protects against premature death

Rationale: The purpose of an annuity is to protect against loss of income arising from excessive longevity. This is exactly the opposite of the purpose of a life insurance policy, which furnishes protection against loss of income arising out of premature death.

492. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.11] What must a fund meet to be included under CPFIS?

  • a. Only a minimum size
  • b. CPFIS admission criteria, including being in the top quartile of the global peer group and benchmarking total expense ratios to the CPFIS median
  • c. Only a MAS licence
  • d. Only a credit rating

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. CPFIS admission criteria, including being in the top quartile of the global peer group and benchmarking total expense ratios to the CPFIS median

Rationale: For funds to be included under CPFIS and thus eligible to take in CPF monies, they must meet the CPFIS funds admission criteria, which include being in the top quartile of global peer group and benchmarking total expense ratios to the median of CPFIS funds in the respective risk class.

493. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §9.11.1] An investor invests $60,000 at $5.00 per share, buying 12,000 units. Using dollar cost averaging over 12 months at an average price of $4.622, how many units are bought?

  • a. 11,500
  • b. 12,000
  • c. 12,980
  • d. 13,000

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. 12,980

Rationale: With dollar cost averaging, $60,000 / $4.622 = 12,980 units, against 12,000 units if invested at the start. The average cost of purchase is $60,000 / 12,980 = $4.622, lower than the $5.00 paid for a single lump sum.

494. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.11.1] What is the practical disadvantage of executing dollar cost averaging accurately?

  • a. It incurs higher management fees
  • b. It cannot be used with unit trusts
  • c. It requires market timing skill
  • d. The investor will likely end up with odd lots

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. The investor will likely end up with odd lots

Rationale: Unfortunately, executing dollar cost averaging accurately will cause the investor to likely end up with odd lots.

495. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.11.1] If an investor missed the best 25 days of the S&P 500 between 1970 and 2016, what happened to the annualised return?

  • a. It fell from 6.7% to 3.0%
  • b. It rose from 3.0% to 6.7%
  • c. It was unchanged
  • d. It fell to zero

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. It fell from 6.7% to 3.0%

Rationale: If he missed investing in the best 25 days of the S&P, his return falls from 1,910% to just 371%, or from 6.7% to 3.0% annually. This is even lower than the return on a 1-month US T-bill rolled over regularly, which would generate an average yield of 4.9%.

496. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.11.1] What proportion of active fund managers have been found to underperform the market each year?

  • a. 50–60%
  • b. 70–80%
  • c. 10–20%
  • d. 30–40%

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. 70–80%

Rationale: Most working adults do not have the time, expertise or emotional temperament to be disciplined active managers of their own investment portfolio, and 70–80% of active fund managers have been found to underperform the market each year.

497. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.13.1] For taxation purposes, how is a Variable Capital Company treated?

  • a. As tax-exempt
  • b. As a partnership
  • c. As a company and a single tax entity
  • d. As a trust

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. As a company and a single tax entity

Rationale: For taxation purposes, a VCC is a company and a single tax entity. A VCC also does not need to publicly disclose its shareholder's register, thus offering investor privacy.

498. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.13.2] What are the two axes of the CPF Investment Risk Classification System?

  • a. Interest rate risk and currency risk
  • b. Market risk and operational risk
  • c. Credit risk and liquidity risk
  • d. Equity risk and focus risk

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Equity risk and focus risk

Rationale: The Risk Classification System splits the investment risk associated with a unit trust into two axes: equity risk and focus risk. Equity risk relates to the proportion of riskier investments; focus risk reflects how focused the investments are in one region, country, industry or company.

499. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §9.13.2] Which unit trust would have higher focus risk?

  • a. A Japan-only equity fund
  • b. A regional Asia fund
  • c. A balanced global fund
  • d. A global equity fund

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. A Japan-only equity fund

Rationale: A unit trust that invests in shares of companies from around the world would have lower focus risk, that is more broadly diversified, than a unit trust that invests in shares of Japanese corporations only. The Japan-only unit trust would have higher focus risk because it is less diversified.

Chapter 10 — Warrants

500. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.1.1] What is a warrant?

  • a. An obligation to buy securities at a fixed price
  • b. A right, but not an obligation, to buy or sell a specified number of securities at a fixed price within a specified period
  • c. A share of ownership in a company
  • d. A bond convertible into cash

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. A right, but not an obligation, to buy or sell a specified number of securities at a fixed price within a specified period

Rationale: A warrant is a derivative instrument that gives the holder the right, but not the obligation, to buy or sell a specified number of securities, usually the issuer's common share, at a fixed price within a specified time period.

501. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.1.1] Why are warrants issued by corporations as a sweetener?

  • a. To avoid regulation
  • b. To increase the coupon rate
  • c. To obtain financing at a lower interest rate, reducing financing cost
  • d. To reduce the number of shares

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. To obtain financing at a lower interest rate, reducing financing cost

Rationale: Warrants are generally issued by corporations as a sweetener, attached to an offering of bonds or rights issues. This allows the issuer to obtain financing at a lower interest rate, hence reduce its financing cost.

502. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.1.1] How does exercising a warrant differ from exercising an equity call option?

  • a. Both cause dilution
  • b. Neither causes dilution
  • c. Warrants cause no dilution; options do
  • d. Warrants cause dilution because new shares are issued; options cause no dilution

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Warrants cause dilution because new shares are issued; options cause no dilution

Rationale: When the warrants are exercised, new shares will be issued, increasing the number of shares outstanding and resulting in a dilution of the issuer's earnings among its shareholders. This differs from equity call options, which do not cause earnings dilution because no new shares are issued.

503. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.1.2] Which of the following is a difference between company warrants and structured warrants?

  • a. Company warrants are call only; structured warrants can be calls or puts
  • b. Company warrants are cash-settled; structured warrants deliver shares
  • c. Company warrants have shorter maturities
  • d. Company warrants can be puts; structured warrants are calls only

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Company warrants are call only; structured warrants can be calls or puts

Rationale: Company-issued warrants are one direction only, that is call warrants. Structured warrants can be both call and put warrants. Company warrants are settled with delivery of shares; structured warrants are cash-settled.

504. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.1.2] In the structured warrant code CapitalaMBeCW181009, what does the "e" represent?

  • a. The issuer
  • b. The exercise style, European
  • c. The expiry date
  • d. The warrant type

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. The exercise style, European

Rationale: In the code, Capitala is the underlying asset, MB is the issuer Macquarie Bank, e represents a European style warrant, CW represents a Call Warrant, 181009 is the expiry date in YYMMDD format, and COWW is the warrant code.

505. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.1.2] What is the typical maturity of a structured warrant compared with a company warrant?

  • a. Both 6–12 months
  • b. Both measured in years
  • c. Structured 6–12 months; company measured in years
  • d. Structured measured in years; company 6–12 months

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Structured 6–12 months; company measured in years

Rationale: Company warrants have a maturity typically measured in years. Structured warrants have a shorter-term maturity of usually 6–12 months.

506. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.1.3] How is the exercise price typically set when warrants are issued?

  • a. Equal to the prevailing share price
  • b. At par value
  • c. Below the prevailing share price
  • d. Above the prevailing share price

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Above the prevailing share price

Rationale: Typically, when warrants are issued, the exercise price or strike price is set above the prevailing share price.

507. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.1.3] What does the conversion ratio of a warrant state?

  • a. The number of warrants required for the conversion to one share
  • b. The ratio of premium to intrinsic value
  • c. The gearing ratio
  • d. The number of shares outstanding

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. The number of warrants required for the conversion to one share

Rationale: The conversion ratio states the number of warrants required for the conversion to one share.

508. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §10.2.1] A warrant has a share price of $0.48, an exercise price of $0.43 and a conversion ratio of 1. What is its intrinsic value?

  • a. $0.00
  • b. $0.05
  • c. $0.43
  • d. $0.48

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. $0.05

Rationale: Intrinsic value = (SP − EP) / n = (0.480 − 0.430) / 1 = $0.05. When the share price is below the exercise price the warrant is out-of-the-money and intrinsic value is zero.

509. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §10.2.2] A warrant trades at $0.030 with an exercise price of $0.430 and a conversion ratio of 1. What is the conversion price?

  • a. $0.40
  • b. $0.43
  • c. $0.46
  • d. $0.49

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. $0.46

Rationale: Conversion price = n × WP + EP = 1 × 0.030 + 0.430 = $0.46. This is the total price the investor has to pay to convert warrants into one underlying share.

510. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §10.2.3] A warrant trades at $0.030, the exercise price is $0.430 and the share price is $0.26. What is the warrant premium in percentage terms?

  • a. 16.54%
  • b. 76.92%
  • c. 6.25%
  • d. 11.54%

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. 76.92%

Rationale: Warrant premium ($) = nWP + EP − SP = 0.030 + 0.430 − 0.26 = $0.20. Premium (%) = 0.20 / 0.26 × 100 = 76.92%.

511. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §10.2.3] A warrant trades at $0.080, the exercise price is $0.430 and the share price is $0.480. What is the warrant premium in percentage terms?

  • a. 30.00%
  • b. 76.92%
  • c. 6.25%
  • d. 16.67%

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. 6.25%

Rationale: Warrant premium ($) = 0.080 + 0.430 − 0.480 = $0.030. Premium (%) = 0.030 / 0.480 × 100 = 6.25%. The premium falls sharply as the warrant moves in-the-money.

512. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.2.3] In the DF Int'l example, the share price rose 85% while the warrant price rose 167%. What does this illustrate?

  • a. That the warrant was overpriced
  • b. That the conversion ratio changed
  • c. That warrants are less risky than shares
  • d. The leverage effect, offering greater percentage gains and losses than the underlying share

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. The leverage effect, offering greater percentage gains and losses than the underlying share

Rationale: Investors are interested in warrants mainly because of the leverage effect. Leverage offers the potential for greater percentage gains and losses than the underlying share for a given change in the price of the underlying share.

513. [Calculation] [CM-EIP §10.2.4] A share price is $0.265 and the warrant price is $0.030 with a conversion ratio of 1. What is the gearing ratio?

  • a. 8.8x
  • b. 0.11x
  • c. 88x
  • d. 1.9x

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. 8.8x

Rationale: Gearing ratio = SP / (n × WP) = 0.265 / 0.030 = 8.8x approximately. Gearing gives investors the ability to participate in the performance of the underlying share at a lower price.

514. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.2.5] For a warrant with a conversion ratio of 1, what is the maximum value?

  • a. The intrinsic value
  • b. The underlying share price
  • c. The exercise price
  • d. The warrant premium

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. The underlying share price

Rationale: For a warrant with a conversion ratio of 1, the maximum value of a warrant is the underlying share price. Most warrant prices do not trade at their maximum value and prices fall as the warrants approach expiration.

515. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.2.5] For a warrant with a conversion ratio of 1, what is the minimum value?

  • a. The exercise price
  • b. Zero always
  • c. The intrinsic value, which is SP − EP if SP > EP and zero otherwise
  • d. The share price

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. The intrinsic value, which is SP − EP if SP > EP and zero otherwise

Rationale: For a warrant with a conversion ratio of 1, the minimum value of a warrant is the intrinsic value of the warrant: SP − EP if SP > EP, and zero if SP is less than or equal to EP.

516. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.2.5] Why does the premium of the warrant price over intrinsic value decrease as the share price rises well above the exercise price?

  • a. Because the warrant expires
  • b. Because the conversion ratio changes
  • c. Because the exercise price falls
  • d. Because the leverage potential declines, with a given share price increase constituting an increasingly smaller percentage of the share price

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Because the leverage potential declines, with a given share price increase constituting an increasingly smaller percentage of the share price

Rationale: As the price of the underlying share increases beyond a point, the premium of the warrant price over the intrinsic value decreases. This is because the leverage potential of the warrant declines at some point, with a given share price increase constituting an increasingly smaller percentage of the share price.

517. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.3] How does an increase in the remaining life of a warrant affect its price?

  • a. It increases the price
  • b. It has no effect
  • c. It makes the price zero
  • d. It reduces the price

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. It increases the price

Rationale: Other things being equal, the longer the time before the warrants expire, the greater the warrant price. A warrant that is out-of-the-money may become attractive later as the share price appreciates. On the day of expiration, warrants are worth their intrinsic value, after which they are worthless.

518. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.3] How does an increase in the exercise price affect a warrant's price?

  • a. It increases the price
  • b. It reduces the price
  • c. It has no effect
  • d. It doubles the price

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. It reduces the price

Rationale: The lower the exercise price, the greater the warrant price. The ability to convert warrants into a share by paying a lower exercise price increases the intrinsic value of the warrant and hence the warrant's price.

519. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.3] Why does higher volatility of the underlying share increase a warrant's price?

  • a. Because volatility extends the maturity
  • b. Because volatility increases the exercise price
  • c. Because warrants benefit from price upside but limit price downside
  • d. Because volatility reduces the conversion ratio

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Because warrants benefit from price upside but limit price downside

Rationale: The more volatile the price of the underlying share, the higher the warrant price, because warrants benefit from price upside but limit price downside. Volatility can change over time.

520. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.3] Why do dividend declarations reduce a call warrant's price?

  • a. Because dividends reduce volatility
  • b. Because dividends shorten the warrant's life
  • c. Because dividends increase the exercise price
  • d. Because warrant holders are not entitled to dividends, so call warrants lose out

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Because warrant holders are not entitled to dividends, so call warrants lose out

Rationale: As warrant holders are not entitled to dividends, the declaration of dividends by the company will reduce the call warrant's price slightly, since call warrants lose out.

521. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.3] Why do higher interest rates increase a call warrant's price?

  • a. Because of interest savings from the lower capital needed to buy warrants compared with an equivalent long stock position
  • b. Because volatility rises
  • c. Because dividends fall
  • d. Because the exercise price falls

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Because of interest savings from the lower capital needed to buy warrants compared with an equivalent long stock position

Rationale: The higher the interest rate, the higher the call warrant price due to interest savings from the lower capital needed to buy warrants compared to an equivalent long stock position. For completeness, the higher the interest rate, the lower the put warrant price.

522. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.3] Why is there a negative relation between the size of a warrant issue and the warrant price?

  • a. Because larger issues are less liquid
  • b. Because more warrants outstanding means larger potential earnings dilution if the warrants are deep in-the-money
  • c. Because larger issues have shorter maturities
  • d. Because larger issues have lower exercise prices

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Because more warrants outstanding means larger potential earnings dilution if the warrants are deep in-the-money

Rationale: The more warrants a company has outstanding, the larger the potential earnings dilution of the existing shares. If the warrants were deep in-the-money, the conversion of the warrants would increase the number of outstanding shares and reduce the value of each share. Thus there is a negative relation between the size of the warrant issue and the warrant price.

523. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §10.4] How are company warrants settled compared with structured warrants?

  • a. Both deliver shares
  • b. Both are cash-settled
  • c. Company warrants are settled with delivery of shares; structured warrants are cash-settled
  • d. Company warrants are cash-settled; structured warrants deliver shares

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Company warrants are settled with delivery of shares; structured warrants are cash-settled

Rationale: Company warrants are settled with a delivery of shares. Structured warrants are cash-settled, that is no delivery of shares.

Chapter 11 — Technical Analysis and Quantitative Analysis

524. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.1.2] What does technical analysis study?

  • a. The economy and industry structure
  • b. Financial statements
  • c. A company's business profitability
  • d. Historical market data such as price changes and trading volume, to forecast price fluctuations

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Historical market data such as price changes and trading volume, to forecast price fluctuations

Rationale: Technical analysis refers to the methodology of forecasting fluctuations in securities prices through an examination of historical market data. It is sometimes called market or internal analysis because it studies demand and supply conditions based on market data such as price changes and trading volume.

525. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.1.2] What is the key difference between technical and fundamental analysts regarding irrational factors?

  • a. Technical analysts expect irrational factors to persist; fundamental analysts expect only a temporary effect
  • b. Fundamental analysts expect irrational factors to persist; technical analysts expect only a temporary effect
  • c. Both expect them to persist
  • d. Neither considers them

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Technical analysts expect irrational factors to persist; fundamental analysts expect only a temporary effect

Rationale: A key difference in opinion relates to the influence of irrational factors: technical analysts expect irrational factors to persist while fundamental analysts expect only a temporary effect, with rational beliefs prevailing in the long run.

526. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.1.2] Why do technical analysts believe prices exhibit trends that persist over long periods?

  • a. Because markets are perfectly efficient
  • b. Because new information is priced by the market over a period of time rather than at one point, so prices adjust gradually
  • c. Because all investors have the same information
  • d. Because prices are random

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. Because new information is priced by the market over a period of time rather than at one point, so prices adjust gradually

Rationale: Technical analysts believe that new information affecting supply and demand is priced by the market over a period of time rather than at one point in time, given that there are different sources of information or because some investors have earlier access. As the speed of adjustment is gradual, prices are expected to exhibit trends that persist over long periods.

527. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.1.3] What presents the greatest challenge to technical analysis?

  • a. Behavioural finance
  • b. Fundamental analysis
  • c. The Efficient Market Hypothesis
  • d. The random walk theory alone

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. The Efficient Market Hypothesis

Rationale: The Efficient Market Hypothesis presents the greatest challenge to technical analysis. Studies that tested weak-form market efficiency generally support the notion that prices adjust rapidly to new information, leaving little opportunity for technical analysts.

528. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.1.3] Why might a popular technical trading rule eventually stop working?

  • a. Because regulators ban it
  • b. Because charts are unavailable
  • c. Because the rule is mathematically wrong
  • d. Because many participants act on it at the same time, so some act first and the resulting competition negates the rule's value

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Because many participants act on it at the same time, so some act first and the resulting competition negates the rule's value

Rationale: If a popular technical analysis trading rule is followed by many market participants, some participants will act first, causing prices to change quicker than expected, so that only those who act earliest will benefit. The popularity and the resulting competition will eventually negate the value of the technical trading rule.

529. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.2.1] Why is a moving average more useful than the daily price for detecting trends?

  • a. Because it includes past prices, making it more stable and smoothing out erratic changes
  • b. Because it predicts the future exactly
  • c. Because it ignores volume
  • d. Because it is more volatile

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. Because it includes past prices, making it more stable and smoothing out erratic changes

Rationale: The moving average is more stable than the daily price because of the inclusion of past prices, making it a more useful measure for detecting trends. If the current market price starts to diverge directionally from the moving average, this may indicate a trend reversal.

530. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.2.2] What does the advance-decline line measure?

  • a. The average price of all shares
  • b. On a cumulative daily basis, the net difference between the number of shares with price advances and those with price declines
  • c. The trading volume of the index
  • d. The volatility of the index

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. On a cumulative daily basis, the net difference between the number of shares with price advances and those with price declines

Rationale: The A/D line measures, on a cumulative daily basis, the net difference between the number of shares that have price advances and those that have price declines. The net advance or decline is calculated by subtracting the number of declines from the number of advances, and the line plots a running total over time.

531. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.2.2] What does it mean if the index is rising while the advance-decline line is falling?

  • a. Volume is increasing
  • b. The market is technically strong
  • c. Most shares are not participating in the rising market, indicating a market peak
  • d. The market is oversold

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. Most shares are not participating in the rising market, indicating a market peak

Rationale: In a rising market, any divergence between the index and A/D line is of concern because it means that most shares are not participating in the rising market, indicating a market peak. Conversely, if the A/D line is rising and the index is declining, it signals a reversal of the falling trend.

532. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.2.3] What are the most commonly used MACD parameters?

  • a. 20, 50, 200
  • b. 14, 30, 7
  • c. 5, 10, 20
  • d. 12, 26, 9

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. 12, 26, 9

Rationale: The most commonly used values are 12, 26 and 9 days, that is MACD (12,26,9). The MACD series is the difference of EMAs with characteristic times a and b, and the average series is an EMA of the MACD series with characteristic time c.

533. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.2.3] What signal is given when the MACD crosses and falls below the signal line?

  • a. A bearish signal indicating it may be time to sell
  • b. A neutral signal
  • c. No signal
  • d. A bullish signal

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. A bearish signal indicating it may be time to sell

Rationale: When the MACD crosses and falls below the signal line, it is a bearish signal that indicates that it may be time to sell. Conversely, when the MACD crosses and rises above the signal line, the indicator gives a bullish signal.

534. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.2.5] What moving average and multiplier are typically used to construct Bollinger Bands?

  • a. A 50-day SMA with a multiplier of 1
  • b. A 20-day SMA with a multiplier of 2
  • c. A 200-day SMA with a multiplier of 3
  • d. A 10-day EMA with a multiplier of 2

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. A 20-day SMA with a multiplier of 2

Rationale: The first step is to compute the simple moving average, typically using a 20-day SMA. Then multiply the standard deviation by two, a commonly used multiplier, and both add and subtract that amount from each point along the SMA.

535. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.2.5] What does it mean when prices move close to the upper Bollinger Band?

  • a. Volatility has fallen to zero
  • b. The market is oversold
  • c. The market is more overbought and may be primed for a trend reversal or price correction
  • d. The market is fairly valued

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. The market is more overbought and may be primed for a trend reversal or price correction

Rationale: Many traders believe the closer the prices move to the upper band, the more overbought the market, and the closer the prices move to the lower band, the more oversold the market. An overbought or oversold market may be primed for a trend reversal or price correction.

536. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.3] What defines an uptrend?

  • a. Current highs below previous highs and current lows below previous lows
  • b. Current highs above previous highs but current lows below previous lows
  • c. A flat price line
  • d. Current highs above previous highs and current lows above previous lows

Answer and explanation

Answer: D. Current highs above previous highs and current lows above previous lows

Rationale: An uptrend is in place when current highs are above previous highs and current lows are also above previous lows. A downtrend occurs when current lows are lower than previous lows and current peaks are also lower than previous peaks.

537. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.3] What is a support level?

  • a. A price range where a substantial increase in demand is expected
  • b. The highest price ever reached
  • c. The average price over a year
  • d. A price range where buyers refuse to pay higher prices

Answer and explanation

Answer: A. A price range where a substantial increase in demand is expected

Rationale: A support level is a price range where a substantial increase in demand is expected. At this level, prices are relatively attractive for buyers who had missed the opportunity to buy earlier.

538. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.3] What is the market overhang?

  • a. Excess demand at a support level
  • b. The supply created by sellers attracted by higher prices to take profits at a resistance level
  • c. The volume traded in a day
  • d. The gap between bid and offer

Answer and explanation

Answer: B. The supply created by sellers attracted by higher prices to take profits at a resistance level

Rationale: As prices increase, a resistance level develops where buyers refuse to pay higher prices. At the same time, sellers may be attracted by higher prices to take profits on their holdings. This supply is called the market overhang.

539. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.3] What is a breakout below a support level?

  • a. A continuation of the uptrend
  • b. A bullish signal
  • c. A bearish signal indicating a reversal of the uptrend
  • d. A neutral signal

Answer and explanation

Answer: C. A bearish signal indicating a reversal of the uptrend

Rationale: A breakout above the resistance level is a bullish signal, whereas a breakout below the support level is a bearish signal. A penetration below a support line would be a sell signal as it indicates a reversal of the uptrend.

540. [Single choice] [CM-EIP §11.4] What does a price decline accompanied by heavy volume indicate?

  • a. A neutral signal
  • b. A buying opportunity
  • c. A bullish signal
  • d. A very bearish signal reflecting a strong and widespread desire to sell
